It's not a perfect solution, but it's better than either a resting limit order that might not fill or a purely mental stop that might not get executed because the trader was distracted.

Now the re-entry framework, which is where the three positions diverge most sharply and where I think both analysts are most vulnerable. The aggressive analyst's framework, which allows any 2-3 of the five criteria to trigger re-entry, is too permissive because it treats all criteria as equivalent when they're not. RSI below 30 in a 3x leveraged ETF during a selloff is a mathematical near-certainty, not a signal. A bullish reversal candle without volume confirmation is a pattern that fails regularly in leveraged products. The conservative analyst's framework, which requires actual PBOC rate cuts or concrete trade agreements, is too restrictive because it ignores legitimate technical signals that can precede macro confirmation by weeks. Both frameworks share a common flaw, which is that they treat the re-entry as a binary decision triggered by a specific set of conditions. The re-entry should be a process, not an event.

Here's what I mean by that, and this is my core disagreement with both analysts. The aggressive analyst wants to deploy 60-75% of the trimmed position when signals converge. The conservative analyst wants to deploy 25-30% when actual policy changes occur. Both are pre-committing to a specific deployment size based on a specific trigger condition. But the trader doesn't know in advance what the market will look like when the re-entry opportunity arrives. The signal quality might be strong or weak. The macro context might be improving or deteriorating. The price level might be $26.31 or $24 or $21.45. Pre-committing to a deployment size before you know the quality of the setup is like pre-committing to a bet size before you've seen your cards. The deployment size should scale with the quality of the convergence.

Let me be specific. If the re-entry scenario involves a daily TD-9 completion, a bullish reversal candle at $26.31 with volume expansion of 40% above the 5-day average, RSI at 28, and PBOC cut probability ticking up from 20% to 23% over a week, that's a moderate-quality convergence. It has technical support but marginal macro support. In that scenario, the initial deployment should be 30-35% of the trimmed position. If, two sessions later, YINN has held above the reversal candle's low and PBOC probability has continued ticking up to 26%, add another 20-25%. If, instead, YINN breaks below the reversal candle's low, exit the initial tranche and wait.

Contrast that with a scenario where the daily TD-9 completes, a bullish engulfing candle forms at $26.31 on volume 70% above the 5-day average, RSI is at 24, and PBOC cut probability has jumped to 31% on a weak Chinese PMI print. That's a high-quality convergence with both technical and macro support. In that scenario, the initial deployment should be 45-50% of the trimmed position, with a planned second tranche of 20-25% if follow-through confirms over the next 2-3 sessions.

The point is that the deployment size should reflect the quality of the setup, not a pre-committed number. The aggressive analyst's 60-75% makes sense for the high-quality scenario but is reckless for the moderate-quality scenario. The conservative analyst's 25-30% makes sense for the moderate-quality scenario but is excessively timid for the high-quality scenario. The framework should be conditional, not fixed.

And this is where I want to challenge the aggressive analyst's claim that staged re-entry is an emotional comfort blanket. They ran an EV calculation showing that full-position deployment outperforms staged deployment by 3.24 percentage points of expected return, using their invented 70% success rate. But that calculation assumes the trader knows the signal quality in advance. If the trader could know with certainty that the reversal was genuine, then yes, full position is optimal. But the trader can't know that. The staged approach isn't about emotional comfort. It's about information acquisition. The first tranche is a probe that buys you information about whether the signal is genuine. If it is, you add. If it isn't, you exit with a smaller loss. The aggressive analyst's EV calculation treats the signal quality as known, which is the exact assumption that makes their math work and which is also the exact assumption that's false.

The conservative analyst says the cost of caution is measured in basis points of opportunity while the cost of aggression is measured in percentage points of capital. This is a compelling framing, but it's only true if you define aggression as deploying full size on every signal regardless of quality. My framework doesn't do that. It deploys aggressively when signal quality is high and cautiously when signal quality is moderate. The cost of this approach is that you occasionally miss some upside on moderate-quality signals that turn out to be genuine. The benefit is that you avoid deploying full size on false signals, which in a 3x leveraged ETF can produce double-digit losses in a matter of sessions.

On the macro signal question, I want to address the conservative analyst's point about prediction market volatility directly because it's the strongest part of their argument. They correctly note that PBOC cut probability swung 6.5 percentage points in a week, which means a move from 20% to 25% could be noise. This is a real concern. But the conservative analyst's solution, which is to wait for actual PBOC policy changes, throws out the baby with the bathwater. The solution isn't to ignore prediction market shifts. It's to require sustained shifts. A PBOC probability that moves from 20% to 27% over two weeks, with multiple supporting data points like weak PMI prints, declining property sector data, or PBOC official commentary signaling dovishness, is a different signal than a single-day jump from 20% to 25% on a rumor. The conservative analyst is right that individual prediction market readings are noisy. They're wrong that this makes the entire category of expectation-shift signals worthless. The key is to look at the trajectory and the supporting data, not just the level.

So here's my final framework, and I want to be specific because the conservative analyst correctly criticized my previous call for flexibility as vague.

Trim 62-67% of the position at current levels. The remaining 33-38% has a hard stop at $27.00, executed via price alert at $27.42 and market sell at $27.10. A daily close below $27.42 triggers an immediate full exit regardless of where the intraday stop sits.

The re-entry is a conditional process, not a fixed event. It requires at least one macro signal, defined as PBOC cut probability above 25% sustained for at least two consecutive weekly readings, with at least one supporting economic data point, OR trade resolution odds stabilizing for two consecutive weeks after a period of decline. AND at least one high-quality technical signal, defined as a daily TD-9 completion with price holding above the setup level on the following session, OR a bullish reversal candle at or below $26.31 with volume at least 40% above the 5-day average. RSI below 30 alone does not qualify. A reversal candle without volume does not qualify.

Initial deployment size scales with signal quality. Moderate-quality convergence, which meets the minimum thresholds, warrants 30-35% of the trimmed position. High-quality convergence, which exceeds the minimums on multiple criteria, warrants 45-50%. A second tranche of 20-25% can be added after 2-3 sessions of follow-through confirmation. If the initial entry is violated, exit and reassess.

The monthly TD-9 serves as a conviction multiplier. If it's still in effect when the re-entry criteria align, the deployment can be at the higher end of the range for the given quality level. It doesn't change the framework. It amplifies the conviction within the framework.

The sentiment data is weighted at 8-10% as a behavioral overlay. It reinforces the bearish case by suggesting retail holders haven't capitulated, which means there's potential supply ahead. It doesn't change the SELL decision. It shades the urgency slightly.

The PE ratio is weighted at 3-5% as context for the upside scenario. It doesn't affect timing or mechanical performance. It provides a rough ceiling on the magnitude of any potential rally, which is relevant for assessing whether the re-entry risk-reward is favorable.

Both analysts will find things to dislike about this framework. The aggressive analyst will say the thresholds are too restrictive and the deployment sizes are too small. The conservative analyst will say the thresholds are too permissive and the deployment sizes are too large. That's exactly the response a moderate framework should produce, because it means neither extreme is getting exactly what they want.

The aggressive analyst's core error is treating conviction as a substitute for confirmation. They believe in the re-entry thesis so strongly that they want to deploy capital aggressively when signals converge, regardless of whether the convergence is strong or weak. The conservative analyst's core error is treating caution as a substitute for judgment. They're so afraid of false signals that they want to wait for absolute confirmation, regardless of the opportunity cost. The moderate path requires judgment, which is harder than either conviction or caution, but which is the only thing that produces reliable returns in a 3x leveraged ETF over time.

The SELL is correct. Execute it at 62-67%. Set the stop at $27.00. Then monitor the data with discipline and patience, deploying capital conditionally when the quality of the convergence justifies it. Not too fast, not too slow. Not too much, not too little. The middle path is harder to defend because it doesn't offer the aggressive analyst's narrative of bold opportunism or the conservative analyst's narrative of stoic discipline. What it offers is a framework that adapts to reality instead of imposing a pre-committed narrative on it. In a 3x leveraged ETF where reality has a way of destroying narratives on both sides, that's the only sustainable edge. Neutral Analyst: Both of my colleagues have now delivered their final arguments across multiple rounds, and I have to say, what's become clear is that they've each constructed internally consistent frameworks that are each fatally flawed in mirror-image ways. The aggressive analyst has built a re-entry thesis so elaborate that it's become the tail wagging the dog — the SELL has become merely the setup for the buy they're already mentally committed to. The conservative analyst has built a risk management framework so restrictive that it functions as a rationale for never deploying capital again. And both of them have spent more energy arguing about a re-entry that hasn't happened yet than about the SELL that needs to happen now.

Let me start with the aggressive analyst's expected value calculation, because both analysts are now circling this like it's the decisive battleground, and both are getting it wrong in different ways. The aggressive analyst runs the math at a 50-55% success rate and gets a positive 11.16% expected return. The conservative analyst says the 50-55% was an illustrative counterexample, not an endorsed estimate, and accuses the aggressive analyst of manufacturing confidence through false precision. Both are right about the other and wrong about themselves. The honest truth is that none of us has a validated success rate for this specific signal convergence in this specific instrument under these specific conditions. But here's what the aggressive analyst's EV calculation actually reveals, and it's not what they think it reveals. Even if you accept their numbers, the 11.16% expected return depends on the gain being 25-30% and the loss being 5-7%. The gain assumption is based on the June-July rally, which occurred in a different macro regime. The loss assumption is based on a stop that may not fill at the intended level. Both inputs are optimistic. If you adjust the gain down to 15-20% to account for the weaker macro backdrop and the loss up to 7-9% to account for gap risk and slippage, the expected return drops to something like 0.52 times 17.5% minus 0.48 times 8% equals 9.1% minus 3.84% equals 5.26%. Still positive, but substantially less compelling, and sensitive enough to the inputs that reasonable adjustments could push it toward zero. The aggressive analyst presents EV math as if it settles the argument. It doesn't. It just relocates the argument from the conclusion to the assumptions.

Now the conservative analyst's capital impairment argument is actually their strongest point across this entire debate, and the aggressive analyst hasn't adequately addressed it. When a re-entry fails in a 3x leveraged ETF, the loss doesn't just reduce the position — it reduces the capital base for all future trades. The conservative analyst's example of the trader who loses $2,300 on a failed re-entry and then captures $575 less on the next successful trade is mathematically sound. But here's where the conservative analyst overplays their hand. They present this as if it's an argument against re-entering on technical signals at all. It's not. It's an argument against deploying too aggressively on any single re-entry. The capital impairment cost scales with position size. A 30% deployment that stops out loses one-third as much capital as a 90% deployment that stops out, which means the impairment on subsequent trades is one-third as severe. The conservative analyst's own argument actually supports a staged, moderate deployment rather than their preferred alternative of no deployment at all. They've constructed a critique of aggressive sizing and then applied it to the entire category of technical re-entry, which is a non sequitur.

On the June-July rally analogy, I think both analysts are talking past each other in a way that obscures the actual lesson. The aggressive analyst says the rally happened without a confirmed PBOC cut, proving reversals can occur on technicals alone. The conservative analyst says the rally happened when easing expectations were alive, which the current regime lacks. Both are probably partially right, but both miss the deeper point. The June-July rally was a 51.5% move in a 3x leveraged ETF. Even if you attribute half of that to macro expectations and half to technical mean reversion, the technical component alone would have produced a 25% move — which is still substantial. The question isn't whether technical reversals can happen without macro confirmation. They clearly can. The question is whether the probability of a technical reversal producing a meaningful move is high enough to justify deployment without macro support. And the answer to that depends on the quality of the technical setup, which is exactly what my conditional framework attempts to assess. Both analysts are treating the June-July rally as either proof that technicals work or proof that macro was necessary. It's neither. It's a single data point that demonstrates the potential magnitude of a YINN reversal, regardless of the cause.

The conservative analyst says this proves my framework is practically unexecutable. The aggressive analyst says the same thing from the other direction — that the two-week filter causes the trader to miss the initial move.

Here's my response. Both analysts are describing a real problem, but both are proposing the wrong solution. The conservative analyst's solution — wait for actual PBOC policy changes — eliminates the ability to act on technical signals that precede macro confirmation. My two-week filter was an attempt to thread the needle, but both analysts have correctly identified that it can create a paralysis window where strong technical signals fire but the macro confirmation hasn't had time to develop. So let me revise the framework based on their critiques. Instead of requiring two consecutive weekly PBOC readings above 25%, the macro signal can be satisfied by EITHER a sustained shift (two readings above 25%) OR a sharp shift on a specific catalyst (a single reading above 30% triggered by a discrete economic event like a weak PMI print or a PBOC official's dovish commentary). This addresses the conservative analyst's concern about noise — a jump to 30% on a specific catalyst is different from a drift to 26% on no identifiable cause. And it addresses the aggressive analyst's concern about the paralysis window — if the macro shift is sharp enough and catalyst-driven, the trader can act on the first signal rather than waiting for confirmation. The key distinction is between catalyst-driven shifts and drift. Drift requires confirmation. Catalyst-driven shifts don't, because the catalyst itself is the confirmation.

On the weekly TD-9, I want to make what I think is the definitive statement on this signal, because both analysts have been arguing around the edges without addressing the core issue. The monthly TD-9 plus 9 and the weekly TD-9 minus 5 are signals on different timeframes describing different aspects of the same market. Trade tariff resolution odds fell from 6.4% to 4% (-2.4pp). US retail sales posted their worst drop in 14 months. Oil is rallying on Iran tensions, and China is the world's largest oil importer. Japan recession risk sits at 26%, creating regional contagion risk. There is not a single positive macro catalyst in the dataset. The bull's "latent catalyst" framing — that PBOC flexibility due to low inflation could produce a surprise ease — is speculative; the data shows catalysts actively deteriorating.

The 3x daily-reset structure is structurally hostile in the current choppy regime. The last three months demonstrate the destructive pattern: decline (-33.6%), rally (+51.5%), decline (-13.2%). The underlying index was roughly flat while YINN lost 12.9% — pure negative convexity destroying capital. The bull's staged-entry strategy is mathematically flawed for this instrument: the daily reset means existing positions bleed via volatility decay while waiting for add levels. As the conservative analyst demonstrated, averaging down in a 3x leveraged ETF is fundamentally different from averaging down in a stock.

The monthly TD-9 +9 is a legitimate but unconfirmed watch signal. All three analysts correctly classified it as context-setting, not actionable. It has been complete for multiple sessions during which YINN has declined 13.2%. The weekly TD-9 is building a sell setup at -5 of 9, and the daily TD-9 is only at +4 of 9. The signal remains unconfirmed and is being actively contradicted by near-term price action. Expected value is negative by all calculations. The bull's own EV yielded -1.37% for a 30% position. The bear's comprehensive five-scenario calculation yielded -1.515% before non-linear decay. The bull's argument that the cost of being early (1.35%) is less than the cost of waiting (1.5%) was based on a miscalculation — the full probability-weighted cost of being early exceeds the cost of waiting when all scenarios are included and the average decline is correctly computed at 14.2% rather than 10%.

Position sizing rationale: The consensus trim of 62-70% reflects the overwhelming bearish evidence while maintaining a residual 30-38% with a tightly bounded stop at $27.00 ($1.17 below current price, a 4.1% decline corresponding to roughly 1.4% in the underlying). The stop could trigger in a single session — YINN moved 4.4% on August 11 — but this is acceptable as it strictly bounds maximum residual loss. The conservative analyst's point about gap risk and slippage is well-taken: the stop should be executed as a mental stop with market order, not a resting limit order, to ensure the exit fills. The neutral analyst's two-alert system (alert at $27.42, execute at $27.10) provides a brief tactical assessment window.

Re-entry discipline: Re-entry should be a conditional process requiring convergence across categories, not a pre-committed event. At least one sustained or catalyst-driven macro signal AND one high-quality technical signal must align. The aggressive analyst's willingness to deploy on any 2-3 technical signals alone is too permissive given the elevated false-positive rate in leveraged ETFs. The conservative analyst's insistence on waiting for actual PBOC policy changes is too restrictive given that the PBOC meets on a schedule with an 82% probability of inaction at the next meeting — the trader could sit idle for months while a genuine reversal occurs on technical convergence. The moderate framework — requiring cross-category confirmation with quality thresholds — balances these risks.
